ctrl=gen_bez(P,u,t1,t2); e,i=maxerr(ctrl,P,u)\\n            if e<err: out.append(ctrl); return\\n    i=max(1,min(len(P)-2,i))\\n    tc=unit(P[i-1]-P[i+1])\\n    fit(P[:i+1],t1,tc,err,out); fit(P[i:],-tc,t2,err,out)\\n# ---------- contour processing ----------\\ndef resample(C,step):\\n    C=np.vstack([C,C[:1]])\\n    d=np.r_[0,np.cumsum(np.linalg.norm(np.diff(C,axis=0),axis=1))]\\n    n=max(8,int(d[-1]/step))\\n    s=np.linspace(0,d[-1],n,endpoint=False)\\n    return np.c_[np.interp(s,d,C[:,0]),np.interp(s,d,C[:,1])]\\ndef corners(P,k=4,ang=38):\\n    n=len(P); idx=np.arange(n)\\n    a=P-P[(idx-k)%n]; b=P[(idx+k)%n]-P\\n    cosang=(a*b).sum(1)/(np.linalg.norm(a,axis=1)*np.linalg.norm(b,axis=1)+1e-9)\\n    turn=np.degrees(np.arccos(np.clip(cosang,-1,1)))\\n    cs=[]\\n    for i in range(n):\\n        if turn[i]>ang and turn[i]>=max(turn[(i+j)%n] for j in range(-k,k+1)):\\n            if not cs or i-cs[-1]>k: cs.append(i)\\n    return cs\\ndef contour_segments(P,err=1.6):\\n    n=len(P)\\n    cs=corners(P)\\n    segs=[]\\n    if not cs:\\n        # closed smooth: split at topmost point and fit\\n        i0=int(np.argmax(P[:,1])); P=np.roll(P,-i0,axis=0); cs=[0]; smooth_closed=True\\n    else: smooth_closed=False\\n    cs=sorted(cs)\\n    for j,c in enumerate(cs):\\n        c2=cs[(j+1)%len(cs)]\\n        if c2<=c: c2+=n\\n        seg=P[np.arange(c,c2+1)%n]\\n        segs.append((seg, smooth_closed))\\n    out=[]\\n    for seg,sc in segs:\\n        if len(seg)<2: continue\\n        # straight?\\n        a,b=seg[0],seg[-1]; L=np.linalg.norm(b-a)\\n        if L>1e-6:\\n            dev=np.abs(np.cross(b-a,seg-a))/L\\n            if dev.max()<1.8:\\n                out.append(('L',a,b)); continue\\n        if sc:\\n            t=unit(seg[1]-seg[-2]); t1=t; t2=-t\\n        else:\\n            m=min(3,len(seg)-1)\\n            t1=unit(seg[m]-seg[0]); t2=unit(seg[-1-m]-seg[-1])\\n        cub=[]; fit(seg,t1,t2,err,cub)\\n        for c in cub: out.append(('C',)+tuple(c))\\n    return out\\nEOF\\necho ok\"}"

Here's where things stand.\n\n**What I learned from the two signs**\n\nComparing the sign letters with the 1966 sheets, all scaled to the same x-height, the narrow version isn't just a squeezed standard alphabet:\n\n- **Lighter strokes.** Vertical stems are about 0.215 of the x-height on both signs, against about 0.26 on the 1966 sheets. That's roughly 16% lighter. Horizontal strokes are lighter by about the same amount.\n- **Uneven narrowing.** Straight-sided letters (n, h, u) and diagonals (w, y) come out at about 78% of standard width. Round letters (o, e) only narrow to about 87%. The r and t keep most of their arm and crossbar length (about 91\u201395%).\n- **Different shapes, not just proportions.** On the Bayshore sign, the a has a smaller, teardrop-shaped bowl. The w has rounded inner points that sit higher, and the y's descender is about 24% deeper than on the 1966 sheets.\n- **Two consistency checks.** The ascender on the sign's h matches the 1966 proportion, at about 1.30 of the x-height. Letter spacing on the signs is generous: about 0.33 of the x-height between straight sides and about 0.22 between round ones.\n\n**How the draft font is built**\n\n- I traced **a, e, h, o, r, s, w, y** directly from the Bayshore Fwy sign, which is sharp enough. The o has a rust patch at the top right, so I rebuilt it by mirroring its clean bottom-left quarter.\n- The Business District photo is too blurry to trace cleanly. Its u, n, c, i and t do match the narrowed 1966 shapes closely, though, so I used it to set their widths.\n- I made the **other 18 letters** from the 1966 sheets. I cleaned the grid lines off the scans and straightened each page, then narrowed each letter by its own factor fitted to the sign letters. I thinned the strokes to the sign weight, made the i and j dots round, and set ascenders and descenders to match the sign h and y.\n- The draft is a 1000-unit TrueType font with an x-height of 525, an ascender of 680 and descenders of about \u2212230. It has a space and spacing based on what I measured on the signs.\n\n**Still wrong in the current draft**\n\n- The k's leg doesn't join the arm properly; there's a gap.\n- The curve fitting overshoots in a few places, mainly the h shoulder and the r's arm.\n- The traced letters still have slightly wavy edges, and the outlines have more points than they need.\n- Long straight stems sometimes have small steps in them.\n\nAll of these come from my tracing steps rather than the source material, and I know how to fix each one. If you reply \"continue,\" I'll fix them, check every glyph at large size, and deliver the .ttf. I can also add B, D and F traced from the signs, since they appear on both.",

    "type": "text",
    "text": "The finished font is in HighwayNarrow1966-Lowercase.ttf, and specimen.png shows the full alphabet plus \"bayshore fwy\" and \"business district\" set in it.\n\n**The new photos.** The 2008 shots don't show the Business District sign any more clearly than before. They did turn up something useful, though. The Page Mill Rd sign beside it is the same size, but its lowercase is standard width, not narrow. Measured in the same photo, its e is about 20% wider than the Business District e, and its stems are about 25% heavier. So that sign is a standard-width alphabet, and Business District and Bayshore Fwy remain the only examples of the narrow one. It also confirms the narrow version is a separate design rather than a camera or angle effect.\n\n**How the letters were made**\n- **Traced from the Bayshore Fwy sign:** a, e, h, o, r, s, w, y. The o has rust damage, so I rebuilt it by mirroring its clean lower-left quarter.\n- **Narrowed from the 1966 sheets:** the other 18 letters. I narrowed and lightened each one to match the signs:\n  - Stroke weight is about 0.22 of the x-height, against about 0.26 on the sheets.\n  - Straight-sided letters are about 78% of standard width and round ones about 82\u201387%.\n  - The u, n, c, i and t have widths fitted to the Business District letters.\n- **Vertical proportions:** the x-height is 525 units and the ascender is 680, both matched to the sign's h. Descenders are deepened to match the sign's y, which drops further than on the sheets.\n- **Spacing** is taken from the signs, so it's generous, as on the signs themselves. Word space is 300 units.\n\n**Limitations**\n- The k, b, d, l and f come straight from the 1966 shapes, narrowed. In the k, that leaves the leg joining the stem just below the arm rather than meeting at the arm.\n- The traced letters are only as accurate as a roughly 115-pixel x-height allows. Small details, like exactly how the terminals are cut, are my best reading of the photo.\n- The font has lowercase and a space only. I can add B, D and F traced from the signs, plus standard punctuation, if that would help.",
    "citations": []
  }

Since the Bayshore panel is visibly warped and its letters don't sit on a common baseline, I'd count that as within measurement error.\n\n**D: probably Series D, a bit wide.** It comes out about 7% wider and slightly heavier. This is the softest of the photos (cap height is only about 94 pixels). Blur rounds the bowl outward, which is the direction of the difference, but it doesn't fully explain 7%. It's a likely Series D, but less certain than the B.\n\n**F: does not match.** It's about 13% wider and about 20% heavier than the Series D F. The middle bar also sits a little higher and runs longer. The B on the same sign, photographed at the same resolution and angle, measures close to Series D. That makes it unlikely the camera caused the F's difference. The F looks like it's from a wider, heavier alphabet, perhaps a different series or a replacement letter.\n\nThe overall stroke weight on the signs runs a few percent above Series D for every letter. Some of that is probably glare from the reflective white copy, which makes bright strokes look slightly fatter in photos. So the B's small excess weight is unremarkable, but the F's 20% is not.\n\nIf you have the 1966 Series E sheets for B, D and F, I can run the same overlay. So either glare thickens the strokes by about 10%, or these are Series D shapes cut with a somewhat heavier stroke. Two photos can't settle that.\n\nSo the Bayshore Fwy sign mixes series: a Series D-width B and a Series E F. That could be a replacement F, or a sign shop using what it had on hand. That ratio comes from the B next to the lowercase on both signs.\n- **Weight:** the capitals keep their Series D stroke, about 108 units against the lowercase's 115. They sit together evenly, as the signs show.\n- **Figures:** they're cap height, with proportional widths as on the sheets. I spaced them like the capitals rather than giving them all the same width.\n- **Spacing:** the capitals use the same generous sign spacing as the lowercase. Round sides sit closer than straight ones, and A, V, W, X and Y sit tighter still.\n\nWhile doing this I tightened how curves and straight lines are told apart in the outlines. That stops gentle curves, like the 3's top and the 9's tail, being flattened into straight segments. The lowercase was rebuilt with the same improvement.\n\nThe font still has no punctuation: no period, comma, hyphen, ampersand or slash.     "text": "HighwayNarrow1966C.ttf is the new Series C font: Series C capitals and figures traced from your sheets, plus an extrapolated Series C lowercase. specimen-D-vs-C.png sets the same lines in both fonts, one above the other.\n\nI took \"the same proportions\" to mean that the new lowercase should relate to the narrow sign lowercase the way Series C capitals relate to Series D capitals. So I measured all 36 capitals and figures on both sets of sheets and applied those ratios.\n\n| | Series C \u00f7 Series D |\n|---|---|\n| Width, most letters and figures | 0.80\u20130.83 |\n| Width, A and Y | 0.74\u20130.75 |\n| Width, W and X | 0.87\u20130.88 |\n| Vertical stems | 0.91 |\n| Horizontal bars | 0.93 |\n\n**How the lowercase was narrowed**\n- **Widths:** each letter takes the ratio of its closest capital. Straight-sided letters get 0.81, rounds 0.82, k 0.79, v 0.80, w 0.87, x 0.88, y 0.78 and z 0.81. The i, j and l follow I's 0.90.\n- **Strokes:** they thin from 115 to 105 units, about 0.91, while the counters narrow. That keeps the colour even rather than turning the letters dark the way a plain horizontal squeeze would.\n- **Heights:** x-height, ascenders, descenders and cap height are unchanged from the Series D version.\n- **Spacing:** I tightened sidebearings and the word space to 85% of the Series D version, so the narrower letters don't look loosely set. That figure is my judgment; the sheets don't specify it. I can change it easily.\n\n**Fix to both fonts.** The scans leave tiny bumps where grid lines met letter edges, which showed as small dents on the 0, O and C. I smoothed those out along each edge without rounding the corners, then rebuilt both fonts. HighwayNarrow1966.ttf is that updated Series D version.\n\nThe Series C lowercase is an extrapolation. No sign or sheet shows what a narrow Series C lowercase actually looked like, so treat it as a plausible reconstruction rather than a recovered design. The Series C capitals and figures, by contrast, are straight tracings of the 1966 sheets.",

It replaces the extrapolated extra-narrow lowercase I made earlier.\n- I lightened the lowercase to a 93-unit stem against the Series C capitals' roughly 100. That keeps the same lighter-lowercase relationship you approved before; at its previous weight it would have looked dark next to these lighter capitals.\n- The lowercase keeps its full sign spacing. The capitals keep the tighter Series C spacing, with your P and S adjustments.\n\n**HighwayNarrow1966D.ttf (Highway Narrow 1966 D)**\n- Series D capitals and figures with a new semi-condensed lowercase.\n- Each letter's width is halfway between the 1966 standard lower case and the narrow sign lowercase. Series D sits about midway between Series E and Series C in both width and weight, so halfway is the matching step for the lowercase. The stem is 100 units against the capitals' 108, the same relationship as the other font. Descenders are halfway between the 1966 sheets and the deeper sign y.\n- All 26 letters are built from the 1966 standard shapes, narrowed to these widths. That includes the eight letters traced from the signs. So this font's a, e, s, w, y and others follow the 1966 sheets rather than the sign variants, such as the sign's smaller-bowled a and round-pointed w.\n\nI removed the old HighwayNarrow1966.ttf to avoid mixing it up with these two. HighwayNarrow1966-Lowercase.ttf, the first lowercase-only version, is also out of date now.\n\nIf you'd rather the semi-condensed lowercase use the sign shapes for those eight letters, I can widen the sign tracings to the same halfway widths instead.",

The y's descender is set 10% shallower than on the sign, to sit halfway like the other descenders.\n\n**Differences in the specimen**\n- **a:** the sign a has a smaller, more teardrop-shaped bowl and a rounder top. It's the most noticeable change.\n- **w:** the sign w's inner points are rounded rather than sharp, and sit a little higher.\n- **y:** the sign y's arms are slightly curved, and it has a longer, more upright tail.\n- **h:** its ascender top is cut nearly flat, where the 1966 h is sharply slanted. Next to the slanted tops of b, d, k and l, the h is the one letter that looks a little out of family.\n- **e, s, o, r:** only small differences in terminals and curve fullness. These are hard to spot at text sizes.\n\nOverall the sign forms read slightly softer and rounder; the 1966 forms are crisper and more geometric.\n\nBecause these eight letters come from photos, they carry some unevenness that the sheet-traced letters don't. The h's two stems, for instance, differ by about 15%. That's barely visible in the specimen but could show at very large sizes.",
    "citations": []
  }

    "text": "That's a fair read, and it points to a real weakness in how I built my letters.\n\nI made each condensed letter by squeezing the 1966 shape sideways, then correcting the weight with horizontal and vertical adjustments. That keeps the straight stems right but distorts the curves:\n- **Shoulders and bowls** (n, h, a, o): where a curve turns from vertical to horizontal, the diagonal parts of the stroke come out thinner than the stems. That gives a slightly pinched, stretched look.\n- **Diagonals** (v, w, y): squeezing makes them steeper, which thins them perpendicular to the stroke even though their horizontal width is correct.\n\nThe sign letters were drawn as narrow letters from the start, so the stroke stays about the same thickness all the way around each curve.\n\nI see two ways to get both sharpness and that even stroke:\n\n1. **Clean up the sign letters.** Square their corners, straighten and even out the stems (the h's two stems, for instance), and sharpen the terminals. This only covers the eight letters on the signs; the other 18 would still be squeezed.\n2. **Rebuild every lowercase letter with an even stroke.** I'd find each 1966 letter's centre line, narrow that line rather than the outline, then redraw the stroke at a constant width along it. Square ends and corners would be restored afterwards, so they don't come out rounded. Curves, shoulders and diagonals would all keep the same thickness, as on the signs, and all 26 letters would be consistent with each other.\n\nOption 2 is more work but would fix the issue throughout both fonts, including the extra-narrow letters in the Series C font, which were squeezed even harder. I could then use option 1 alongside it for the eight sign letters.\n\nWould you like me to try option 2 on a few test letters (n, a, o, s, w) first, so you can compare before I redo the whole alphabet?",

The figure is the thinnest point divided by the thickest, so 1.0 would be perfectly even:\n\n| | Current | Even-stroke | Sign forms |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| n | 0.77 | 0.81 | 0.77 |\n| a | 0.73 | 0.79 | 0.62 |\n| o | 0.93 | 0.89 | 0.77 |\n| s | 0.77 | 0.78 | 0.70 |\n\nThe even-stroke method makes the n's shoulder and the a's bowl a little fuller, but the gain is small. By this measure the sign letters are actually the least even of the three.\n\n**Why it made little difference.** The Series D lowercase is only 2\u20138% narrower than the 1966 standard, so my squeeze barely distorted it. What you're noticing in the sign letters isn't more even strokes but a different drawing. Their curves are fuller and rounder: the a's bowl, the n's shoulder, the s's rounded ends and the w's rounded inner points. The 1966 shapes are flatter-sided and more geometric, so narrowed even slightly they read as squeezed, even though the strokes measure about the same.\n\n**Where even strokes would really help** is the Series C font. Its 18 lowercase letters not on the signs were squeezed to about 78% width. That's enough to visibly thin the curves and diagonals, and they sit next to the eight sign letters.\n\nFor the Series D font, if the sign letters' character is what you like, using the sign shapes for all 26 letters would get closer than any stroke correction. That would mean reshaping the 18 letters not on the signs to match the sign letters' round shoulders and full bowls.\n\nWould you rather I apply the even-stroke rebuild to the Series C font's 18 squeezed letters, or work on giving the Series D lowercase the sign letters' rounder shapes throughout?",
